NOTICE 
Notice is hereby given that Thirty-second (32nd) Annual General Meeting of Asian Star Company Limited will be held on 
Monday, September 28, 2026 at 2.00 p.m. through Video Conferencing (“VC”)/ Other Audio Visual Means (“OAVM”) to transact 
the following business. The venue of the meeting shall be deemed to be the Registered Office of the Company at 114-C, 
Mittal Court, Nariman point Mumbai 400 021 
 
ORDINARY BUSINESS: 
1. To receive, consider and adopt: 
a.  the audited standalone financial statements of the Company for the financial year ended March 31, 2026 together with 
the Reports of the Board of Directors and Auditor's thereon and 
b. the audited consolidated financial statements of the Company for the financial year ended March 31, 2026 together 
with the Reports of the Auditor's thereon. 
2. To declare a dividend of Rs. 1.50 per Equity share, for the year ended March 31, 2026. 
3. To consider and appoint Mr. Priyanshu A. Shah (DIN: 00004759) as a Director, who retires by rotation and being eligible, 
offers himself, for re-appointment. 
4. To consider and appoint Mr. Dharmesh D. Shah (DIN: 00004704) as a Director, who retires by rotation and being eligible, 
offers himself, for re-appointment. 
 
Place: Mumbai 
Date: August 13, 2026 
ByOrderoftheBoardofDirectors

NOTES 
 
The Ministry of Corporate Affairs (“MCA”), vide its General Circular No. 20/2020 dated 5 May 2020 and 17/2020 dated April 
13, 2020, in relation to “Clarification on passing of ordinary and special resolutions by companies under the Companies Act, 
2013”, General Circular Nos. 20/2020 dated 5 May 2020 and subsequent circulars issued in this regard, the latest being 
General Circular No 03/2025 dated 22 September 2025 in relation to “Clarification on holding of AGM through VC/OAVM, 
collectively referred to as “MCA Circulars”, has allowed companies to conduct the Annual General Meeting(“AGM”) through 
Video Conferencing (“VC”) or Other Audio-Visual Means (“OAVM”) at a common venue without physical presence of members. 
In compliance with the provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 (the Act), SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015, as amended, (Listing Regulations) and MCA Circulars, the 32nd AGM of the Company is being 
conducted through VC/OAVM at the registered office of the Company as the deemed venue. 
 
1. The relevant details of the Director seeking re-appointment at this Annual General Meeting, as required under Regulation 
36(3) of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 and Secretarial Standard on 
General Meetings (SS-2) issued by the Institute of Company Secretaries of India, are annexed to this Notice. 
 
2. Since this AGM is being held pursuant to the MCA circulars through VC/OAVM, physical attendance of Members has been 
dispensed with. Accordingly, the facility for appointment of proxies by members will not be available for the AGM and hence, 
Proxy Form, Attendance Slip and Route Map are not annexed to this Notice.

3. Authorized representatives of the corporate members/institutional investors intending to participate in the AGM pursuant 
to Sec 113 of the Act, are requested to send to the Company certified copy of Board Resolution along with Authority letter etc. 
authorizing them to attend the AGM, by email to secretarial@asainstargroup.com or upload on the VC portal / e-voting portal 
not later than 48 hours before the scheduled time of the commencement of the Meeting. 
 
4. The Members can join the AGM in the VC/OAVM mode 15 minutes before and after the scheduled time of the 
commencement of the Meeting by following the procedure mentioned in the Notice. The large Shareholders 
(Shareholders holding 2% or more shareholding), Promoters, Institutional Investors, Directors, Key Managerial Personnel, 
the Chairpersons of the Audit Committee, Nomination and Remuneration Committee and Stakeholders Relationship 
Committee, Auditors etc. who are allowed to attend the AGM without restriction on account of first come first served basis. 
 
5. The attendance of the Members attending the AGM through VC/OAVM will be counted for the purpose of reckoning the 
quorum under Section 103 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
 
6. Pursuant to the provisions of Section 108 of the Companies Act, 2013 read with Rule 20 of the Companies (Management 
and Administration) Rules, 2014 (as amended)and Regulation 44 of SEBI (Listing Obligations & Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations 2015 (as amended), and the Circulars issued by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs dated April 08, 2020, April 
13, 2020 and May 05, 2020 the Company is providing facility of remote e-Voting to its Members in respect of the business 
to be transacted at the AGM. For this purpose, the Company has entered into an agreement with National Securities 
Depository Limited (NSDL) for facilitating voting through electronic means, as the authorized agency. The facility of 
casting votes by a member using remote e- Voting system as well as venue voting on the date of AGM will be provided by 
NSDL. Kindly check instruction for attending AGM. 
 
7. In line with the Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA) and SEBI Circulars, the Notice of the 32nd AGM along with the Annual 
Report 2025-26 are being sent only by electronic mode to those members whose e-mail addresses are registered with 
the Company/Depositories. Members may please note that this notice and Annual Report 2025-26 will also be available 
on the Company's website at https://www.asianstargroup.com/investor-centre/. The Notice can also be accessed from the 
website of the Stock Exchanges i.e. BSE Limited at www.bseindia.com and the AGM Notice is also available on the website 
of NSDL (agency for providing the Remote e-Voting facility) i.e. https://www.evoting.nsdl.com. 
 
8. In case of joint holders only such joint holder who is higher in the order of names will be entitled to vote during the meeting. 
 
9. The Register of Directors and Key Managerial Personnel and their shareholding, maintained under Section 170 of the Act, 
and the Register of Contracts or Arrangements in which the directors are interested, maintained under Section 189 of the 
Act, will be available electronically for inspection by the members during the AGM. All documents referred to in the 
Notice will also be available for electronic inspection without any fee by the members from the date of circulation of this 
Notice up to the date of AGM, i.e. September 28, 2026. Members seeking to inspect such documents can send an email to 
secretarial@asianstargroup.com. 
 
10. The Record date shall be September 21, 2026, for determining the entitlement of Members for dividend for the year ended 
March 31, 2026, if declared. 
Dividend related information 
11. Final dividend for the year ended March 31, 2026, as recommended by the Board of Directors, if declared, at the AGM, 
shall be  paid to those Members, whose names appear: - 
 
a) as beneficial owners at the end of business hours on September 21, 2026, as per lists furnished by Central Depository 
Services Limited and National Securities Depository Limited in respect of shares held in electronic form; and 
 
b) on the Register of Members of the Company as on September 21, 2026, after giving effect to valid transfers in respect of 
transfer requests lodged with Bigshare Services Private Limited., Registrar and Share Transfer Agent (RTA) of the 
Company, on or before the close of business hours on September 21, 2026, in respect of shares held in physical form. 
 
12. Members holding shares in dematerialised form are requested to intimate particulars of bank mandates, nominations, 
power of attorney, e-mail address, contact numbers, change of address, etc. to their Depository Participant (DP). Members 
holding shares in physical form are requested to intimate these details to the RTA. 
 
13. In order to enable the Company to remit dividend electronically through National Electronic Funds Transfer (NEFT), 
Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS), etc., Members holding shares in physical form are requested to provide/ update 
details of their bank accounts indicating the name of the bank, branch, account number and the nine-digit MICR code and 
IFSC code (as appearing on the cheque) along with scanned copy of the cheque/cancelled cheque to 
sandeep@bigshareonline.com. Members holding shares in dematerialised form are requested to provide the said details to 
their DP.

---


## Page 4

14. In case the Company is unable to pay dividend to any shareholder by electronic mode, due to non-availability of the details 
of the bank account, the Company shall dispatch the dividend warrants to such shareholder by post. 
 
15. Pursuant to the Finance Act, 2020, mandates that dividends paid or distributed by Company after April 1, 2020 shall be 
taxable in the hands of the Shareholders and the Company is required to deduct TDS from the dividend paid to the 
Members at prescribed rates under Income Tax Act 2025. In general, to enable compliance with TDS requirements, 
Members are requested to complete and/or update their Residential status, PAN as per IT Act with their Depository 
Participants (DPs). The detail process is explained in  the e-mail sent to the Shareholders. 
 
16. SEBI has mandated the submission of Permanent Account Number (PAN) by every participant in the securities market. 
Members holding shares in electronic form are requested to submit their PAN to their DPs if not submitted earlier. 
Members holding shares in physical form are requested to submit their PAN to the RTA if not submitted earlier. 
 
17. Members who have not claimed/received their dividend paid by the Company in respect for earlier years are requested to 
check with the company's Registrar. Pursuant to the Act read with the Investor Education and Protection Fund Authority 
(“IEPF Rules”) amounts of dividend remaining unclaimed/ unpaid for a period of seven years are required to be 
transferred to the 'Investor Education and Protection Fund'. Further, according to the said IEPF Rules, shares in respect 
of which dividend has not been claimed by the shareholders for 7 (seven) consecutive years or more shall also be 
transferred to the Demat account of IEPF authority. 
 
a) During the financial year 2025-26, the Company has transferred to IEPF, Rs. 2869.50 towards Unclaimed dividend for 7 
years and 12 shares in respect of which dividend has not been claimed by shareholder for 7 consecutive years is 
transferred to IEPF Demat account. 
 
b) The dividend amount and shares transferred to the IEPF can be claimed by the concerned members from the IEPF 
authority after complying with the procedure prescribed under the IEPF rules. The details of the unclaimed dividends 
are also available on the Company's website at www.asianstargroup.com and the said details have also been uploaded on 
the website of the IEPF Authority. Shareholders can however claim both dividend amount and equity shares from the 
IEPF Authority by making an online application in Form No. IEPF-5, the details of which are available on 
www.iepf.gov.in 
 
Others Information 
 
18.Pursuant to SEBI Circular No. SEBI/HO/CFD/ CMD2/CIR/P/2021/11 dated January 15, 2021 read with Circular No. 
SEBI/HO/CFD/CMD1/ CIR/P/2020/79 dated May 12, 2020, Notice of the AGM for the year ended March 31, 2026 are 
being sent electronically to those Members whose e-mail addresses are registered with the DP/RTA and the same are also 
available on the website of the Company www.asianstargroup.com and on the websites of BSE Limited. 
 
19.As per Regulation 40 of the SEBI LODR SEBI Circular no. SEBI/HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_RTAMB/P/CIR/2022/8 dated 25th 
January 2022, as amended, securities of listed companies can be transferred only in dematerialized form, except in case of 
request received for transmission or transposition of securities. To eliminate all risks associated with physical shares 
and for ease of portfolio management, Members holding shares in physical form are requested to consider converting 
their holdings to dematerialized form. Members can contact the Company's Registrar at sandeep@bigshareonline.com in 
for assistance in this regard. 
 
20.SEBI vide its Circular no. SEBI/HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_RTAMB/P/CIR/2022/8 dated 25th January, 2022 has mandated the 
listed companies to issue securities in dematerialised Form only while processing certain prescribed service requests. 
Accordingly, the members are requested to make service request by submitting a duly filled and signed Form ISR 4, the 
format of which is available on the Company's website at www.asainstargroup.com. Members are requested to note that 
any service request would only be  processed after the folio is KYC Compliant. 
 
21. Members holding shares in physical form in single name are advised to avail of nomination facility. As per the provisions of 
Section 72 of the Act, the facility for making nomination is available for Members in respect of shares held by them. 
Members, who have not yet registered their nomination, are requested to register the same by submitting Form No. SH-
13. Nomination form can be downloaded from the website of the Company www. asianstargroup.com or obtained from 
the RTA. Members are requested to submit the said details to their DP in case shares are held in electronic form and to the 
RTA in case shares are held in physical form. 
 
22. Members are requested to:- 
 
a) Quote DP ID and Client ID/Ledger Folio numbers in all their correspondence; 
 
b) Approach the RTA for consolidation of multiple ledger folios into one; and 
 
c) To avoid inconvenience, get shares transferred in joint names, if they are held in a single name and/or appoint a nominee.

INSTRUCTIONS FOR ATTENDING THE AGM THROUGH VC/OAVM: 
 
1. Member will be provided with a facility to attend the AGM through VC/OAVM through the NSDL e-Voting system. 
Members may access the same at https://www.evoting.nsdl.com under shareholders/members login by using the remote 
e-voting credentials. The link for VC/OAVM will be available in shareholder/members login where the EVEN of Company 
will be displayed. Please note that the members who do not have the User ID and Password for e-Voting or have forgotten the 
User ID and Password may retrieve the same by following the remote e-Voting instructions mentioned in the notice to 
avoid last minute rush. Further members can also use the OTP based login for logging into the e-Voting system of NSDL 
 
2. Members are encouraged to join the Meeting through Laptops for better experience. 
 
3. Further Members will be required to allow Camera and use Internet with a good speed to avoid any disturbance during 
the meeting. 
 
4. Please note that Participants Connecting from Mobile Devices or Tablets or through Laptop connecting via Mobile 
Hotspot may experience Audio/Video loss due to fluctuation in their respective network. It is therefore recommended to use 
Stable Wi-Fi or LAN Connection to mitigate any kind of aforesaid glitches. 
 
5. Shareholders, who would like to express their views/have questions may send their questions in advance mentioning 
their name, demat account number/folio number, email id, mobile number at (secretarial@asianstargroup.com). The 
same will be replied by the company suitably. 
 
INSTRUCTIONS FOR REMOTE E-VOTING: 
Pursuant to the provisions of Section 108 of the Companies Act, 2013 read with Rule 20 of the Companies (Management 
and Administration) Rules, 2014 (as amended)and Regulation 44 of SEBI (Listing Obligations & Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations2015 (as amended), read with General Circular numbers 14/2020 dated 8th April, 2020, 17/2020 dated 13th April 
2020 and 20/2020 dated 5th May, 2020, 22/2020 dated June 15, 2020, 33/2020 dated September 28, 2020, 39/2020 dated

read 
with 
Circular 
number 
SEBI/HO/CFD/CMD1/CIR/P/2020/79 issued by the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) dated 12th May, 2020 
issued by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA)Secretarial Standard on General Meetings (SS-2) issued by Institute of 
Company Secretaries of India, the Company is providing facility of remote e-voting to its Members in respect of the business to 
be transacted at the AGM. For this purpose, the Company has entered into an agreement with National Securities Depository 
Limited (NSDL) for facilitating voting through electronic means, as the authorized agency. The facility of casting votes by a 
member using remote e-voting system as well as venue voting on the date of the AGM will be provided by NSDL. The facility for 
voting through poll paper shall also be made available at the venue of the AGM. The members attending the meeting, who have 
not already cast their vote through remote e-voting shall be able to exercise their voting rights at the meeting. The members who 
have already cast their vote through remote e-voting may attend the meeting but shall not be entitled to cast their vote again at 
the AGM. 
 
The Company has appointed Yogesh D. Dabholkar, Practicing Company Secretary (CP No.: 6752) as the Scrutinizer for 
conducting the  remote e-voting and the voting process at the AGM in a fair and transparent manner. 
 
The Scrutinizer shall, immediately after the conclusion of voting at the AGM, first count the votes cast at the AGM, and 
thereafter unblock the votes cast through remote e-voting, in the presence of at least two (2) witnesses not in the employment 
of the Company and make within 48 hours of the conclusion of the meeting a consolidated scrutinizer's report of the votes cast 
in favour or against, to the Chairman or to any Director or any person authorized by the Chairman for this purpose, who shall 
countersign the same. 
 
THE INSTRUCTIONS FOR MEMBERS FOR REMOTE E-VOTING ARE AS UNDER: 
The remote e-voting period begins on Friday, September 25, 2026 at 9:00 A.M. and ends on Sunday, September 27, 2026 
at 5: 00 P.M. The remote e-voting module shall be disabled by NSDL for voting thereafter. The Members, whose 
names appear in the Register of Members / Beneficial Owners as on the record date (cut-off date) i.e. Monday, 
September 21, 2026, may cast their vote electronically. The voting right of shareholders shall be in proportion to 
their share in the paid-up equity share capital of the Company as on the cut-off date, being Monday, September 21, 
2026.

A) Login method for e-Voting and joining virtual meeting for Individual shareholders holding securities in demat mode. 
In terms of SEBI circular dated December 9, 2020 on e-Voting facility provided by Listed Companies, Individual 
shareholders holding securities in demat mode are allowed to vote through their demat account maintained with 
Depositories and Depository Participants. Shareholders are advised to update their mobile number and email Id in their 
demat accounts in order to access e-Voting facility.

Step 2: Cast your vote electronically and join General Meeting on NSDL e-Voting system. 
 
How to cast your vote electronically and join General Meeting on NSDL e-Voting system? 
1. After successful login at Step 1, you will be able to see all the companies "EVEN" in which you are holding 
shares and whose voting cycle and General Meeting is in active status. 
2. Select "EVEN" of company for which you wish to cast your vote during the remote e-Voting period and casting 
your vote during the General Meeting. For joining virtual meeting, you need to click on "VC/OAVM" link placed 
under "Join General Meeting". 
3. Now you are ready for e-Voting as the Voting page opens. 
4. Cast your vote by selecting appropriate options i.e. assent or dissent, verify/modify the number of shares for 
which you wish to cast your vote and click on "Submit" and also "Confirm" when prompted. 
5. Upon confirmation, the message "Vote cast successfully" will be displayed. 
6. You can also take the printout of the votes cast by you by clicking on the print option on the confirmation page. 
7. Once you confirm your vote on the resolution, you will not be allowed to modify your vote.

32   Annual Report 2025 - 26
nd
FY 25–26 demanded agility from every company across the gems and jewellery value chain. The 
industry grappled with a confluence of macroeconomic pressures, including restrained 
discretionary spending in key consuming markets, geopolitical conflicts, US tariff-related 
uncertainty, unprecedented gold price levels, and continued corrections in polished diamond 
prices. Despite this challenging period, the industry did not weaken as sharply, with India's overall 
gems and jewellery exports recording only a marginal decline. Looking ahead, analysts anticipate 
natural diamond supply to shrink gradually even as demand continues to inch forward, setting the 
stage for a structurally tighter market over the longer term.
Against this backdrop, Asian Star manoeuvred through the year with focus and discipline. While 
consolidated revenue declined slightly, the performance reflected the broader realities of the 
industry, including price corrections, softer domestic demand and margin pressure. Exports grew 
meaningfully and now represent a significant share of total sales, reinforcing the strength of our 
international market relationships. Jewellery sales continued their upward trajectory, reflecting 
our enduring design strength and focused product development. Profitability moderated during 
the year; however, careful working capital management, cash flow discipline and a steady 
emphasis on operational efficiency helped preserve business stability.
The regulatory and trade landscape across key markets also evolved during the year. US tariff 
developments affected one of the industry's largest consuming markets, while geopolitical 
tensions in West Asia created intermittent disruption in sentiment and logistics across an 
important trading region. At the same time, heightened expectations around traceability, ethical 
sourcing and compliance continued to reshape the standards of trade. These developments 
reinforced the importance of geographic and portfolio diversification. For Asian Star, these were 
not just headwinds to weather, they were signals to act on.
Our response was one of deliberate, forward-looking progress. As an organised and compliance-
ready player, we are well positioned to turn evolving regulatory expectations into a lasting 
competitive advantage, and our continued investment in supply chain transparency and 
governance reflects that confidence. We invested in design-led development, creating new 
jewellery lines designed to resonate with diverse markets while strengthening our sales channels. 
We deepened partnerships in regions demonstrating growth potential, managed our exposure in 
softer markets with prudence, and advanced initiatives in traceability and talent development, 
each reinforcing our broader commitment to responsible and sustainable growth.
As we reflect on FY 25–26, we recognise that the year tested the industry's strength and 
adaptability. Yet our experience manoeuvring these challenges has sharpened our resolve and 
strengthened our preparedness for what lies ahead. With a clearer focus, disciplined execution and 
confidence in the long-term fundamentals of the industry, we are better equipped to build on our 
strengths and ready to seize opportunities as the industry evolves.
At Asian Star, we are 
Asian Star Company Limited
Manoeuvring Through Challenges, Committed to Progress

---


## Page 15

DEAR SHAREHOLDERS,
Leading Asian Star through FY 25–26 demanded 
more than operational discipline; it demanded 
conviction, clarity and the willingness to make 
difficult choices. The year tested the judgement of 
every business in our industry, not only through 
external volatility, but through the need to decide 
where to protect, where to invest and where to 
move with greater purpose.
For us, the response was deliberate. We remained 
focused on the long-term direction of the 
business, protected the quality of our decisions in 
a softer demand environment and continued to 
strengthen the areas where Asian Star is best 
positioned for the future.
India's total gems and jewellery exports declined 
3.3% in dollar terms to US$ 27.72 billion for the 
year. Cut and polished diamond exports fell 8.5%, 
while studded gold jewellery, the segment most 
aligned with design and value addition, grew 6.3%. 
These numbers point to an industry in transition, 
where undifferentiated volume is losing ground 
and craftsmanship-led, design-rich products are 
beginning to chart their own course.
Asian Star's performance sat within this reality. 
Our consolidated revenue declined 2.5%, 
reflecting the broader pressures faced by the 
industry. Our diamond revenues declined 10.1%, 
as we made a deliberate choice to protect margins 
and remain prudent in a softer demand cycle. Our 
jewellery division, by contrast, grew 17.4%, 
surpassing the industry's growth in studded 
jewellery and affirming that our investments in 
design, manufacturing capability and customer 
relationships are bearing fruit.
On the domestic front, elevated gold prices and 
cautious consumer sentiment weighed on 
spending, and our domestic sales reflected that 
broader reality. While this moderation was 
expected given the environment, it reinforces our 
continued focus on export markets and design-
driven categories where demand has shown 
greater resilience.
Not every decision this year was easy. In a 
softening cycle, we chose to protect margins over 
market share, pulled back on volumes with 
purpose, and continued investing in our long-
term capabilities even when the immediate 
environment did not demand it. These were 
deliberate choices, made with conviction and 
owned completely. The numbers reflect that 
thinking, but what we value more is the 
organisational discipline and leadership clarity 
that these decisions have reinforced within Asian 
Star.
As we look ahead, we do so with measured 
optimism. While macroeconomic and trade 
uncertainties remain, there are encouraging 
developments that support a more constructive 
outlook. India's improving trade engagement with 
key global markets, along with the possibility of 
further progress on its trade relationship with the 
United States, can support greater market access 
and export competitiveness over time. India's 
strong economic performance and resilient 
growth outlook also provide confidence in the 
broader operating environment.
For Asian Star, the lessons of FY 25–26 have 
sharpened our focus and strengthened our 
preparedness. We move forward with confidence 
in the foundation we have built, the direction we 
have chosen and the people who carry this 
organisation forward every day.
On behalf of the Board, we extend our sincere 
appreciation to our teams, clients, partners and 
shareholders for their continued trust.
Warm Regards,
Vipul P. Shah 
CEO & Managing Director

## Page 16

In 1971, a small cutting and polishing unit in Surat marked the beginning of our journey. Over the five 
decades, our growth has been nothing short of extraordinary – each challenge a stepping stone, each 
achievement a milestone.
Driven by a steadfast dedication to excellence and innovation, coupled with an unwavering commitment to 
our values and mission. Asian Star has built a responsible and high-value vertically integrated powerhouse. 
Our seamless operations encompass ethical sourcing from premier mining companies, in-house diamond 
and jewellery manufacturing, exceptional design and product development that turn concepts into 
successful products and a global marketing network linking us to leading retail brands.
A pivotal moment came in 1996 when we became one of the first companies in India's gems and jewellery 
sector to list on the Bombay Stock Exchange. Yet, this was just the beginning.
Today, standing at the threshold of a new era, we're channelling the invaluable insights gained over half a 
century into driving continuous improvement. By fostering agility and operational efficiency, we're not only 
enhancing our internal processes but also delivering unparalleled value to our customers.

## Page 17

We believe in making a profound impact by contributing to society and using our resources to make a 
meaningful difference. Our focus extends beyond philanthropy, driving societal advancement through 
impactful programs. By collaborating with stakeholders and local communities, we aim to create a more 
inclusive and prosperous society, ensuring opportunities for growth and development are accessible to all. 
Our efforts are concentrated on catalysing positive change across sectors such as education, healthcare, 
women's empowerment, disaster management and environmental conservation.
Caring For People And Planet
Asian Star has cultivated a robust market presence over the years, forming strategic alliances with our 
customers and positioning ourselves as trusted business partners rather than mere suppliers. Our 
collaborative approach, value-added services and unwavering dedication to delivering exceptional 
customer experiences have established us as esteemed partners to prestigious jewellery brands and 
retailers worldwide.
We have built a significant presence across all levels of the diamond supply chain, supported by our  
marketing arms and subsidiaries. Our operations extend to key diamond procurement centres such as 
Antwerp and Dubai, as well as manufacturing hubs in India. Furthermore, our extensive reach spans major 
consumer markets, including the US, Europe, China, and India. This global footprint ensures that we 
remain at the forefront of the industry, consistently delivering unparalleled value and excellence.
Partnerships Beyond Borders
With more than five decades of experience, our skilled artisans, supported by advanced technology, have 
perfected their craft to create polished masterpieces that truly highlight their intrinsic beauty. This 
expertise, combined with our steadfast commitment to maintaining integrity throughout the diamond 
supply chain by adhering to the Responsible Jewellery Council's Code of Practices and ISO 9001: 2015 
standards by TUV NORD, forms the foundation of our product excellence.
Our extensive collection features a diverse array of loose diamonds, both certified and non-certified, 
offered in a variety of shapes, colours and qualities, with sizes up to 5 carats. The Asian Star cut, alongside 
our acclaimed EX-EX-EX Hearts & Arrows cut and other exclusive custom cuts, have set standards in the 
industry. Through strategic alliances with mine-origin programs such as De Beers Forevermark and 
Canadamark, we enhance the value and assurance of our offerings.
Cutting & Polishing Diamonds
Over the past two decades, we have seamlessly integrated diamond jewellery manufacturing into our 
operations, elevating our position in the value chain and significantly enhancing our portfolio. Our 
capabilities are exemplified by a dedicated innovation centre, cutting-edge technology, and a talented 
workforce committed to pioneering new styles. 
We have established a strong presence in the bridal, fashion, and classic diamond jewellery segments, 
while also diversifying into promising new areas such as ceramic fine jewellery and men's jewellery 
featuring Cuban designs. Our commitment to innovation and excellence has transformed us from a mere 
manufacturer to a powerhouse, offering custom jewellery and private labels, making us the supplier of 
choice for leading jewellery brands and retail chains worldwide.
Diamond Jewellery Manufacturing
At the culmination of our integrated value chain, our jewellery boutique offers a selection of ready-to-wear 
diamond jewellery. We also provide personalized consultations to design and create bespoke pieces 
tailored to individual preferences and special occasions.

MANAGEMENT DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS
ECONOMIC OVERVIEW 
Global Economy
FY 2025–26 unfolded against a global economic backdrop defined by uneven growth, elevated policy uncertainty and rapidly shifting 
trade conditions. While economic activity was sustained across major markets, the pace of expansion fell short of earlier projections. 
The International Monetary Fund placed global growth at 3.5% for 2025 and projected a modest further moderation to 3.0% in 2026, 
reflecting the cumulative weight of tariff actions, trade fragmentation and softening momentum across several important economies.
Trade policies emerged as one of the most consequential variables shaping the global environment during the year. Tariff 
announcements, particularly from the United States, introduced significant uncertainty into business confidence, supply chain 
planning and shipment timing. Organisations operating across borders were compelled to reassess sourcing strategies, pricing 
structures and market priorities as duty regimes and trade negotiations continued to evolve. For export-oriented economies and 
globally integrated industries, the net effect was a more fragmented and cautious operating environment.
The World Trade Organization projected that global goods trade growth would slow materially, from 4.6% in 2025 to 1.9% in 2026, with 
tariff-related effects, geopolitical risks and transport disruptions collectively weighing on the medium-term outlook.
Geopolitical developments added further layers of complexity to an already uncertain environment. The ongoing Russia-Ukraine 
conflict continued to exert pressure on energy and commodity markets, while escalating tensions in West Asia affected trade 
sentiment, disrupted shipping routes and elevated energy-linked costs. Disruptions across the Red Sea corridor and the broader 
Middle East region exposed the fragility of global logistics networks to regional instability, with direct consequences for freight 
movement, insurance costs, delivery timelines and overall trade confidence.
Commodity price volatility remained a defining feature of the operating environment. Oil, gold and other key commodities experienced 
sharp price movements through the year, influencing inflation expectations, consumer behaviour and business planning across 
geographies. Energy-linked volatility carried particular significance for production costs, logistics and transportation. The IMF noted 
that oil price trajectories remained acutely sensitive to geopolitical developments and continued access through critical maritime 
routes, including the Strait of Hormuz.
In aggregate, FY 2025–26 reflected a global economy that remained functional but increasingly cautious and less predictable in its 
trajectory. Growth was uneven, trade flows became more sensitive to policy decisions, and the range of external risks that businesses 
were required to manage widened considerably. The year reinforced the strategic value of disciplined planning, diversified market 
exposure, resilient supply chains and agile response mechanisms in navigating a more complex global operating environment.
Indian Economy
India delivered a standout economic performance in FY 2025–26, with real GDP growth at 7.7% for the year. This outcome was 
underpinned by sustained domestic demand, accelerating investment activity, agricultural recovery, robust construction and 
continued public expenditure on infrastructure. Against a global backdrop marked by trade uncertainty, commodity volatility and 
geopolitical disruption, India's growth trajectory reinforced its standing as one of the world's most resilient large economies.
A defining feature of the year was a meaningful moderation in inflation. Consumer price pressures eased considerably through FY 
2025–26, supported by softer food prices, favourable supply conditions and the subsequent disinflationary impact of GST rate 
rationalisation. Retail inflation fell to 0.25% in October 2025, the lowest reading in the current CPI series, creating a more stable price 
environment relative to the preceding year and providing a supportive foundation for consumption and investment decisions.
Domestic demand continued to anchor economic activity, though growth patterns varied across segments. Rural demand strengthened 
on the back of improved farm output, while urban consumption remained selective across categories. Ongoing investments in 
infrastructure, logistics and digital public infrastructure continued to support formalisation, productivity and economic connectivity. 
Public capital expenditure remained a significant driver of growth, with private investment showing measured improvement in select 
sectors.

## Page 20

these headwinds, the industry's overall export performance reflected a narrower decline relative to the sharper corrections recorded 
in specific product categories, pointing to a gradual but discernible shift in the composition of exports. 
India's total gems and jewellery exports declined 3.32% to US$ 27.72 billion during FY 2025–26. A significant portion of this decline was 
attributable to disruption in the United States market, where India's gems and jewellery exports fell 45% to US$ 5.09 billion following 
tariff-related measures. Given the United States' position as one of the most consequential consumer markets for the sector, this 
disruption materially affected shipment planning, buyer sentiment and export momentum through the year.
West Asia also emerged as a critical area of concern towards the latter part of the year. Escalating tensions involving Iran, Israel and the 
United States affected regional trade sentiment and created operational disruption for flows linked to the Middle East. Flight 
cancellations, airspace closures and interruptions in Dubai-linked cargo movement affected both exports and the import of rough 
diamonds. Given Dubai's role as a major hub for polished diamonds, rough diamonds and bullion, the disruption carried particular 
significance for India's gems and jewellery sector, elevating uncertainty around shipment timing, logistics availability, insurance costs 
and buyer confidence.
Within the export basket, performance varied considerably across categories. Cut and polished diamonds remained under pressure, 
reflecting weak global demand and price correction. Jewellery exports provided a comparatively steadier base, with stronger traction 
in value-added and design-led products. Silver and platinum jewellery recorded notable growth, demonstrating that demand remained 
resilient in categories offering affordability, differentiated positioning or stronger end-use relevance.
The year also underscored the strategic importance of market diversification. While exports to the United States declined sharply, 
destinations including the UAE, Hong Kong, Australia and Canada delivered stronger growth. Shipments to the UAE grew by over 10% 
during the year despite geopolitical disruptions, supported by established trade relationships and sustained regional demand. This 
performance reinforced the imperative for Indian exporters to broaden their market base and reduce concentration risk in any single 
destination.
FY 2025–26 marked a period of meaningful transition for India's gems and jewellery industry. The decline in overall exports, sustained 
pressure in diamonds, disruption in the United States market and West Asia-linked logistics challenges collectively reflected the 
complexity of the operating environment. At the same time, growth in value-added jewellery categories and select export markets 
demonstrated that momentum remained available where product positioning and market selection were well aligned.
Cut and Polished Diamonds
The cut and polished diamond segment continued to face pressure during FY 2025–26, though the rate of decline moderated relative to 
the previous year. India's exports of cut and polished diamonds declined 8.52% to US$ 12.16 billion, accounting for 43.9% of total gems 
and jewellery exports. This followed a sharper 16.8% decline in FY 2024–25, when exports had fallen to US$ 13.3 billion. While the 
segment remained under pressure, the more moderate pace of decline indicated an early degree of stabilisation following the 
significant correction of the prior year.
The sustained pressure reflected subdued demand in key overseas markets, continued softness in polished diamond prices and 
cautious inventory management across the midstream. The United States and China remained the most consequential demand 
variables for the trade. Tariff-related uncertainty in the United States affected shipment planning and buyer confidence during the year, 
and given its position as a primary end-consumer market for diamond jewellery, this uncertainty continued to constrain order visibility 
for Indian exporters.
Rough diamond procurement reflected a more disciplined posture during the year. Gross imports of rough diamonds declined 
approximately 2% to US$ 10.47 billion in FY 2025–26, compared with a considerably sharper reduction in the prior year. This indicates 
more selective procurement behaviour rather than a broad withdrawal from manufacturing activity, with processors aligning rough 
buying more closely to confirmed demand and polished price movement, rather than building speculative inventory positions.
Lab-grown diamonds continued to influence pricing dynamics and consumer comparison across the broader diamond market. 
Polished lab-grown diamond exports declined 10.55% to US$ 1.13 billion amid price compression, demonstrating that pricing pressure 
was not confined to natural polished diamonds alone. Loose polished stones across categories faced headwinds, reinforcing the 
importance of clearer differentiation, stronger value communication and a more deliberate emphasis on rarity, origin and assurance in 
natural diamonds.
India's external sector navigated a more complex global environment. Merchandise exports faced headwinds from softer demand in 
key markets, tariff-related uncertainty and evolving global sourcing patterns. Services exports and remittances, however, continued to 
provide meaningful support to India's external position. India's continued pursuit of trade agreements and deeper economic 
partnerships with key markets also aims to provide an important counterbalance to tariff-related pressures, improving the medium-
term prospects for market access and export competitiveness. The current account deficit stood at a manageable 0.6% of GDP for FY 
2025–26, reflecting underlying external resilience despite elevated global volatility.
Policy emphasis on infrastructure, manufacturing capacity, digitalisation, logistics efficiency and formalisation continued to support 
the operating conditions for organised businesses and India's medium-term growth foundation.
Looking ahead, India is well positioned to remain among the stronger performing major economies, though the pace of expansion will 
continue to be shaped by the interplay of domestic demand, private investment momentum, export performance and global conditions. 
FY 2025–26 reaffirmed the importance of productivity, disciplined execution and organisational agility in responding effectively to an 
evolving domestic and external environment.
Industry Overview
Global Gems and Jewellery Industry
FY 2025–26 was defined by a clear divergence between value and volume across the global gems and jewellery industry. Precious 
metals attracted strong investor interest, while jewellery consumption came under pressure in several major markets as prices moved 
sharply upward. The year was therefore characterised less by broad demand expansion and more by how consumers, retailers and 
manufacturers navigated elevated prices, increasingly selective spending patterns and meaningful shifts at the category level.
Gold stood at the centre of this movement. Global gold demand reached record levels in 2025, driven primarily by investment demand 
through exchange-traded funds, bars and coins. Jewellery demand, however, contracted in 2025, declining 18% in volume during the 
year as elevated prices weighed on consumer buying volumes. This contrast illustrated that while gold's relevance as a store of value 
strengthened considerably, its role in jewellery consumption became notably more price sensitive.
This shift was particularly pronounced in China, where gold jewellery consumption declined sharply and bars and coins overtook 
jewellery purchases for the first time, reflecting an increasingly investment-oriented approach to gold ownership. Across markets, 
retailers and manufacturers responded by pivoting towards lighter formats, sharper price-point management and collections 
designed to preserve design appeal without relying solely on metal weight.
The diamond segment remained under sustained pressure through the year. Natural diamonds continued to reflect subdued trading 
sentiment, cautious inventory behaviour and softness in polished prices across parts of the midstream. At the same time, lab-grown 
diamonds experienced steep price compression, especially in accessible price categories. Together, these developments sharpened 
consumer comparison across diamond categories and reinforced the need for clearer differentiation around rarity, origin, emotional 
value and long-term desirability.
Against this backdrop, the natural diamond industry intensified its focus on category distinction and consumer communication. The 
Luanda Accord, supported by major diamond-producing nations, committed 1% of annual diamond sales revenue from participating 
producers towards the promotion of natural diamonds. This represented a coordinated industry-wide effort to reinforce the narrative 
around natural origin, rarity and enduring value, rather than allowing the category to be defined primarily by price comparison.
In aggregate, FY 2025–26 was characterised by selective demand, sustained pricing pressure and a sharper divide between 
investment-led buying and jewellery consumption. Gold retained its prominence as an asset class, but elevated prices constrained 
jewellery volumes. Diamonds continued through a difficult pricing cycle, while the sharp decline in lab-grown diamond prices 
reinforced the need for clearer positioning across categories. The year underscored that future growth will depend on disciplined 
pricing, lighter and more wearable product formats, stronger category storytelling and the ability to align with consumers who are 
increasingly conscious of both value and authenticity.
Indian Gems and Jewellery Industry
India's gems and jewellery industry navigated a demanding year in FY 2025–26, shaped by global trade uncertainty, tariff-related 
disruption, elevated precious metal prices, geopolitical tensions in West Asia and continued pressure in the diamond segment. Despite

Jewellery is expected to remain better positioned where products combine design, craftsmanship and price-point discipline. The 
stronger performance of studded gold jewellery, silver jewellery and platinum jewellery during FY 2025–26 signals that buyers 
responded to differentiated products with clear end-use appeal, reinforcing the importance of design capability, product development, 
quality assurance and customer-specific collections.
Market diversification has moved from strategic priority to operational necessity. The events of FY 2025–26 exposed the cost of 
concentration, whether in a single export market or a single logistics corridor. Building a genuinely balanced market footprint is no 
longer aspirational; it is foundational.
India enters this phase from a position of structural strength, with unmatched diamond processing scale, deep jewellery manufacturing 
capability and a craftsmanship heritage that few industries can replicate. The opportunity now lies in converting this foundation into 
higher value realisation, stronger compliance and products that command preference. Organised, well-governed players with the 
discipline to execute and the agility to adapt are well placed to lead what comes next.
Company Overview
Founded in 1971, Asian Star Company Limited has built an enduring presence in the global diamond and jewellery industry over more 
than five decades. The Company's standing rests on a consistent commitment to precision craftsmanship, ethical sourcing and the 
cultivation of long-term partnerships across the value chain. Through a vertically integrated business model, Asian Star oversees every 
stage of the diamond and jewellery journey, from the procurement of rough diamonds through in-house cutting and polishing, fine 
jewellery manufacturing, global distribution and retail, ensuring quality, traceability and accountability at each step.
Headquartered in Mumbai, the Company's international footprint spans the world's most significant trade and consumption centres, 
including New York, Chicago, Antwerp, Dubai, Shanghai, Hong Kong, Bangkok and Singapore, complemented by key manufacturing and 
distribution presence in Surat, Ahmedabad, Hosur and Chennai. This geographic reach supports operational agility, enables proximity 
to both supply and demand, and reinforces Asian Star's position as a trusted connector across the global diamond pipeline.
Rough supply is sourced directly from leading mining operations worldwide, and the Company holds recognised manufacturer status for 
several prominent global jewellery brands. Its production ecosystem combines skilled craftsmanship with advanced manufacturing 
infrastructure, underpinned by a rigorous approach to quality, governance and sustainability. Asian Star's adherence to ISO 9001:2015 
standards, certified by TUV NORD, and its compliance with the Responsible Jewellery Council's Code of Practices reflect a deep 
commitment to pipeline integrity and responsible business conduct across all levels of its operations.
Manufacturing Facility for Polished Diamond
Asian Star's diamond manufacturing operations are anchored at its Surat facility, which spans 100,000 square feet and is home to over 
1,000 skilled artisans. The facility specialises in the cutting and polishing of diamonds across a wide range of shapes, sizes, colours and 
clarities up to 5 carats, supported by advanced technology that enables both precision and scalability. Its defining strength lies in the 
seamless integration of artisanal expertise with technological capability, producing stones of consistently high quality that are 
internationally recognised under the 'Asian Star Make' benchmark.
The product portfolio encompasses certified diamonds, specialised cuts, programme-based cutting and proprietary creations including 
EX-EX-EX Hearts and Arrows and custom-designed fancy shapes. These diamonds are distinguished not only by their optical excellence 
and symmetry but also by the consistency of output that enables clients to compete with confidence across diverse markets and price 
points. The Company's mine-origin programmes and exclusive cut offerings provide further differentiation, deepening client 
relationships and reinforcing market relevance.
Every stage of production is governed by rigorous quality control and end-to-end traceability systems, ensuring that ethical sourcing 
and sustainability remain central rather than incidental to operations. Through continuous investment in process efficiency, design 
integration and responsible manufacturing practices, the Surat facility sustains Asian Star's position as a benchmark in global diamond 
manufacturing.
Despite this challenging environment, India retained its central position in the global diamond value chain. The country continues to 
process nine out of every ten diamonds handled globally, underpinned by a skilled workforce, significant manufacturing depth and a 
well-established trading ecosystem. India also remained an important demand market in its own right, with domestic consumption 
showing early signs of recovery and industry commentary pointing to India as a constructive demand anchor, alongside improving 
momentum in the Middle East.
Recovery in cut and polished diamonds will require more than a rebound in export volumes. Stability in polished prices, improved 
demand from key consumer markets, balanced rough procurement, responsible sourcing, robust traceability and sharper positioning 
of natural diamonds will all be essential to restoring confidence across the value chain. Manufacturers with disciplined inventory 
management, strong customer relationships and the capability to supply consistent, well-aligned assortments are expected to be 
better positioned as the segment progresses through this adjustment cycle.
Jewellery
Jewellery exports represented a comparatively stronger area of performance within India's gems and jewellery industry during FY 
2025–26, though momentum within the category was uneven. Overall gold jewellery exports remained broadly stable at US$ 11.36 
billion during the year. Within this headline figure, plain gold jewellery declined 7.42%, while studded gold jewellery grew 6.27%, 
reflecting a clear and consequential shift in buyer preference towards value-added and design-led jewellery.
The divergence between plain and studded gold jewellery carried significant implications in a year defined by elevated gold prices. 
Plain gold jewellery, where purchase decisions are more directly tied to metal weight, was considerably more exposed to price 
sensitivity. Studded gold jewellery, by contrast, offered a broader value proposition through design, craftsmanship, stone setting and 
finished-product differentiation. Its growth during a year of overall export moderation demonstrated that buyers remained receptive to 
jewellery where value was created beyond the underlying metal.
The broader jewellery data also indicated that demand was becoming more selective rather than uniformly weak. Silver and platinum 
jewellery recorded strong traction during the year, signalling that buyers remained open to broader material choices where products 
offered either a more accessible entry point or a differentiated premium proposition.
A similar dynamic was visible in the contrast between loose stones and finished jewellery. While loose polished stones remained 
exposed to pricing pressure, finished jewellery performed with greater resilience when supported by design context, affordability and 
clear end-use relevance.
FY 2025–26 demonstrated that jewellery performance was increasingly determined by the depth of value addition. Products dependent 
primarily on metal weight or loose-stone value faced greater price sensitivity, while categories anchored in design, craftsmanship, 
product differentiation and finished jewellery application delivered more resilient results. For organised manufacturers, this 
reinforced the strategic importance of design capability, quality assurance, responsible sourcing, customer-specific collections and 
the ability to deliver jewellery that balances price, wearability and market relevance.
OUTLOOK
The industry is entering a more demanding phase, where growth will depend increasingly on value addition, supply-chain readiness 
and considered market selection. FY 2025–26 demonstrated that demand cycles are no longer the sole pressure point. Tariff 
uncertainty, geopolitical disruptions, elevated precious metal prices and logistics risks have become central considerations for 
business continuity and sustained trade confidence.
Trade access is expected to become a more constructive factor for the industry. India's recent and anticipated trade agreements with 
key markets, including the India-UK Free Trade Agreement and ongoing engagement with the United States, can support demand, 
improve export competitiveness and provide greater market access over time.
In diamonds, restoring confidence remains the critical priority. Stable polished prices, balanced rough procurement and disciplined 
inventory management will be essential to improving movement across the value chain. Natural diamonds will require more 
compelling positioning around rarity, provenance, responsible sourcing and traceability, as buyers place increasing weight on 
assurance and long-term value.

stood at Rs. 63.81 crore compared to Rs. 80.91 crore in FY 2024–25, while profit after tax was Rs. 40.42 crore against Rs. 43.19 crore in

While revenue and profitability reflected the weight of the operating environment, the Company remained focused on disciplined cost 
management, prudent inventory control and selective business execution throughout the year. The sustained growth in jewellery, a 
higher export contribution and a consistent emphasis on operational discipline provide a sound foundation from which Asian Star 
moves forward through an evolving industry cycle.
OPPORTUNITIES
India as a Preferred Global Sourcing Destination
India's standing as a global manufacturing hub for diamonds and jewellery is strengthening, underpinned by evolving trade access, 
improving infrastructure and a fundamental shift in how international buyers approach sourcing. As global supply chains become more 
selective and compliance-driven, India's depth in diamond processing and jewellery manufacturing positions it as a credible, scalable 
and responsible sourcing destination.
Trade agreements are expanding this advantage meaningfully. The India-UK Free Trade Agreement is expected to open a high-value 
consumer market through preferential duty access for Indian gems and jewellery. Existing frameworks with the UAE and EFTA, 
alongside anticipated progress with the EU and Oman, further extend India's reach across both developed and emerging markets, 
providing organised exporters with a structural edge over less-connected competitors.
Domestic infrastructure development is reinforcing manufacturing capability at scale. Initiatives such as the Bharat Ratnam Mega 
Common Facility Centre at SEEPZ, India Jewellery Park Mumbai and Maharashtra's sector-focused manufacturing policy are creating 
conditions for more efficient, transparent and technology-enabled production ecosystems. These developments support the transition 
from fragmented capacity towards organised, cluster-based manufacturing with improved logistics, skill access and compliance 
readiness.
As international buyers place increasing weight on responsible sourcing, traceability and dependable execution, the opportunity for 
organised Indian manufacturers extends well beyond volume. The ability to offer consistent quality, ethical provenance and responsive 
product development positions India as a trusted partner in the global value chain rather than simply a cost-competitive supplier.
Broadening Market Reach Across Geographies
FY 2025–26 reinforced the strategic imperative of a diversified export base. The sharp disruption in the United States market exposed 
the vulnerability of concentration in a single destination, while growth in the UAE, Hong Kong, Australia and Canada demonstrated that 
meaningful demand opportunities exist across a broader geography.
Emerging and underpenetrated markets offer considerable scope for deeper engagement as retail infrastructure matures, luxury 
consumption expands and appetite for design-led jewellery grows. Regions with improving trade access and rising middle-class 
affluence represent a new frontier for Indian exporters prepared to invest in market-specific relationships and product strategies.
Capturing this opportunity requires more than geographic presence. Buyer preferences, price sensitivities, design expectations and 
product formats differ materially across regions. Manufacturers with the capability to adapt assortments to local demand, while 
maintaining consistent quality, compliance and supply reliability, will be best positioned to build enduring customer relationships 
across a wider market footprint.
Diversification Across Precious Metals and Material Categories
The stronger performance of silver and platinum jewellery during FY 2025–26 signals a meaningful broadening of demand across 
material categories, driven by affordability considerations, design evolution and shifting consumer preferences. This creates a tangible 
opportunity for manufacturers to develop differentiated collections that serve both accessible and premium market segments without 
dependence on a single metal category.
Mixed-metal designs, lighter formats and engineered alloys are gaining relevance as buyers seek greater design distinction and wider 
price-point coverage. Manufacturers that invest in multi-material capability and product development across categories will be better 
positioned to serve a more varied and discerning global customer base.
Manufacturing Facility for Jewellery
Asian Star's jewellery manufacturing capabilities are distributed across advanced facilities in Mumbai and Hosur collectively spanning 
over 60,000 square feet with a combined annual production capacity of 750,000 pieces. These facilities form a core component of the 
Company's vertically integrated value chain, designed to serve global markets with precision, scale and the flexibility to respond to 
evolving demand.
The facilities produce a comprehensive range of diamond-studded jewellery across classic, contemporary and fusion aesthetics, 
catering to a broad spectrum of consumer preferences and regional market requirements. Operations combine traditional 
craftsmanship with modern engineering across both handmade and machine-made segments, supported by advanced CAD/CAM 
systems, in-house casting and setting capabilities that enable streamlined workflows, faster turnaround and a high degree of 
customisation.
To strengthen product leadership and design relevance, Asian Star has established a dedicated Product Development Centre focused 
on research-led innovation and market-responsive design solutions. A dedicated design centre further supports the Company's ability 
to anticipate emerging trends and respond to client expectations with agility. Together, these capabilities underpin the creation of 
bespoke collections and private label programmes, positioning Asian Star as a preferred manufacturing partner for leading jewellery 
brands and retail chains across global markets.
Distribution
Asian Star maintains a strategically positioned global network of offices and marketing arms across major manufacturing, trading and 
consumption centres. Supported by the Company's broader organisational workforce, this network provides direct access to real-time 
market intelligence and evolving global design trends, enabling the Company to respond with speed and relevance to shifting client 
needs. The Company's distribution strategy serves a diverse and discerning customer base, encompassing renowned international 
brands, retail chains and trade partners. Underpinning this strategy is an unwavering commitment to placing the customer at the 
centre of every decision, delivering experiences that are personal, consistent and designed to exceed expectations.
Retail
Asian Star operates a dedicated jewellery boutique offering a curated selection of prêt diamond jewellery, designed for high net worth 
individuals seeking pieces that combine exceptional craftsmanship with everyday wearability. The boutique presents thoughtfully 
curated collections of versatile, investment-worthy jewellery alongside a bespoke order service, enabling discerning clients to 
commission personalised creations for significant occasions. Complementing this, Asian Star has extended its retail presence through 
Tryyst, its proprietary fine jewellery label. Rooted in the belief that jewellery should have a soul, Tryyst brings together heritage 
craftsmanship and contemporary design, offering collections that are youthful, design-forward and built on ethically sourced natural 
diamonds. Available across India and the UAE with global shipping, Tryyst represents Asian Star's deliberate step into direct-to-
consumer luxury, taking its manufacturing excellence and translating it into a brand experience that speaks to a new generation of 
jewellery buyers.
Financial Overview
FY 2025–26 presented a demanding operating environment for Asian Star, characterised by sustained pressure in the diamond 
segment, elevated precious metal prices, tariff-related uncertainty and uneven demand across key markets. Against this backdrop, the 
Company maintained a stable operating base, supported by a stronger export contribution and continued momentum in the jewellery 
division.
Consolidated turnover for the year stood at Rs. 2,882.01 crore, compared to Rs. 2,955.75 crore in FY 2024–25, reflecting a measured 
decline of 2.5%. Export sales grew 8.9% to Rs. 1,914.30 crore, accounting for 66.4% of total sales against 59.5% in the prior year, 
underscoring the strength and depth of the Company's international market relationships.
Segment performance reflected the prevailing industry environment. Revenue from the diamond segment declined 10.1% to Rs. 
1,916.56 crore from Rs. 2,132.80 crore in FY 2024–25, in line with broader industry trends. The jewellery segment, by contrast, delivered 
growth of 17.4%, with revenue rising to Rs. 965.45 crore from Rs. 822.40 crore, reaffirming the Company's strategic focus on design-
led, value-added jewellery and the continued strength of this vertical.
Profitability moderated during the year, consistent with margin pressure and a softer operating environment in diamonds. EBITDA

Technology-Led Transformation and Artificial Intelligence
Technology is rapidly becoming a defining competitive lever across the gems and jewellery value chain. Artificial intelligence, digital 
workflows and data-led planning tools are enabling faster design development, enhanced production visibility, more disciplined 
inventory management and sharper customer responsiveness across the industry.
For jewellery manufacturers, AI-enabled tools can meaningfully accelerate the product development cycle, supporting design 
exploration, trend interpretation, assortment planning and collection visualisation. They can also deepen market understanding by 
analysing consumer preferences, regional buying behaviour and product performance across geographies, enabling more targeted 
and commercially viable design decisions.
Across operations, technology can improve planning accuracy, quality assurance, production tracking and material optimisation. As 
margins remain under pressure and demand grows increasingly selective, digital integration offers manufacturers a credible pathway 
to reducing inefficiencies, improving turnaround and aligning production more closely with confirmed customer requirements.
Evolving Consumer Preferences
Global jewellery consumption is undergoing a structural shift, with consumers moving away from purely occasion-led purchasing 
towards pieces that offer everyday wearability, personal expression and enduring design relevance. While price sensitivity remains a 
consideration, buyers are increasingly seeking jewellery that is versatile, lifestyle-aligned and reflective of individual identity.
This evolution creates a compelling opportunity for manufacturers with strong design interpretation capability and the agility to 
translate changing consumer behaviour into commercially viable collections. Lighter formats, modular designs, contemporary styling 
and market-specific aesthetics are expected to gain sustained relevance across both developed and emerging markets. Manufacturers 
that can consistently deliver jewellery combining visual impact, wearability and design distinction will be well placed to capture this 
evolving demand.
RISKS, THREATS AND CONCERNS
Trade Policy Disruption and Geopolitical pressure
The global operating environment for gems and jewellery has become increasingly sensitive to shifts in trade policy, tariff structures, 
import regulations and geopolitical developments. FY 2025–26 demonstrated that disruption is no longer confined to demand; it now 
directly affects shipment planning, buyer confidence, cargo movement, insurance costs and delivery timelines. For an export-led 
industry with globally distributed sourcing and distribution linkages, sudden changes in market access or trade routes can materially 
alter order flows, execution certainty and business continuity planning.
Commodity Price Volatility and Demand Uncertainty
The industry remains exposed to significant price volatility across gold, silver, platinum & polished diamond. Price corrections in 
diamonds affect inventory valuations, margin structures and order visibility, while sharp movements in precious metal prices influence 
consumer demand patterns, product pricing strategies and price-point planning. In such conditions, business performance depends 
not only on revenue growth, but on disciplined procurement practices, careful stock rotation and close alignment between inventory 
positions and confirmed demand.
Evolving Consumption Patterns and Generational Shifts
Jewellery consumption patterns are evolving at an accelerating pace across generations and markets. Younger consumers 
increasingly prioritise everyday wearability, personal expression, design relevance, responsible sourcing and value transparency, 
while more traditional demand remains anchored in occasions, gifting and investment-led purchasing. The principal risk lies in product 
offerings becoming misaligned with how consumers now discover, evaluate and acquire jewellery, with implications for sell-through 
rates, retailer confidence and the long-term health of demand creation across categories.
Competitive Intensity, Margin Pressure and Category Distinction
The industry continues to operate in an environment of intense competition across both diamonds and jewellery. Price-led competition, 
cautious retailer procurement, evolving product preferences and rising operational expectations constrain pricing power and 
compress margins. The steep price correction in lab-grown diamonds has also complicated consumer comparison within the broader 
diamond category, making clear value communication and product differentiation increasingly important.
INTERNAL CONTROLS
Asian Star Company Limited maintains a robust and comprehensive internal control framework, designed to support the integrity of 
financial reporting, operational oversight and regulatory compliance across its business operations. The framework is calibrated to the 
scale and complexity of the Company's activities and encompasses detailed policies and procedures spanning governance, risk 
management, information security, regulatory compliance and audit practices.
These controls are structured to identify irregularities in a timely manner, facilitate prompt corrective action and reinforce 
accountability at every level of the organisation. The effectiveness of the framework is evaluated through regular internal audits and 
periodic risk assessments, which are reviewed by the Audit Committee of the Board in coordination with the internal audit function. 
These reviews are conducted at regular intervals to assess the adequacy of controls, maintain transparency, minimise exposure to 
fraud and ensure continued adherence to statutory obligations.
For the financial year under review, the internal control systems functioned effectively and no material weaknesses were identified in 
either their design or implementation.
HUMAN RESOURCES
Asian Star's people remain central to its performance, culture and long-term growth. The Company's human resources framework is 
built around creating a transparent, inclusive and performance-oriented work environment that supports the professional 
development and wellbeing of its workforce. Engagement, capability and organisational alignment are treated as ongoing priorities 
rather than periodic initiatives.
During the year, the Company continued to invest in structured learning and development programmes, interactive capability-building 
sessions and targeted workshops across quality, production and operational functions. These initiatives are designed to nurture a 
growth-oriented culture and equip employees with the skills required to meet evolving business demands.
Employee satisfaction and motivation are supported through clearly defined reward and recognition programmes and consistent 
acknowledgement from leadership. These efforts ensure that the Company's workforce remains skilled, engaged and aligned with 
Asian Star's values and strategic direction as the organisation continues to grow and evolve.

Financial Performance of the Company
FY 2025–26 presented a demanding operating environment for the Industry, characterised by geopolitical issues, tariff-related 
uncertainty, elevated precious metal prices, and uneven demand across key markets. Against this backdrop, the Company maintained a 
stable operating base, supported by a stronger export contribution and continued momentum in the jewellery division.
Consolidated turnover for the year stood at Rs. 2,882.01 crore, compared to Rs. 2,955.75 crore in FY 2024–25, reflecting a measured 
decline of 2.5%. Standalone turnover was Rs. 2205.74 crore against that of 2336.37 crore with a decline of 5.60%. Profitability 
moderated during the year, consistent with margin pressure and a softer operating environment in diamonds. 
While revenue and profitability reflected the weight of the operating environment, the Company remained focused on disciplined cost 
management, prudent inventory control and selective business execution throughout the year. The sustained growth in jewellery, a 
higher export contribution and a consistent emphasis on operational discipline provide a sound foundation for the Company to move 
forward through an evolving industry cycle.
Dividend
Based on the company's performance, the Board recommended a final dividend on equity shares at the rate of 15% (i.e. Rs. 1.50 per 
Equity Share of Rs. 10/- each) which will be Rs. 240.10 Lakhs subject to the approval of the members at the ensuing Annual General 
Meeting would be paid to members whose name appears in the Register of Members as on the Book Closure date. 
Pursuant to Finance Act, 2020, dividend income is taxable in the hands of the shareholders w.e.f. 1st April, 2020 and the Company is 
required to deduct tax at source (TDS) from dividend paid to the members at prescribed rates as per the Income-tax Act, 2025. The 
Dividend Distribution policy for your company is available on the website of your Company, weblink of which is given below:
https://www.asianstargroup.com/wp content/themes/appwaychild/pdfs/cg/2320Dividend%20Distribution%20Policy.pdf
Transfer to Reserves
During FY 2025-26, the Board of your Company has not recommended transfer of any amount to reserves and has decided to retain the 
entire amount of profits for Financial Year 2025-26 in the profit and loss account.

Corporate Governance Report
Your Company has always been devoted to adopting and adhering to the best Corporate Governance practices. The Company 
understands and respects its fiduciary role and responsibility towards stakeholders and society at large and strives hard to serve their 
interests, resulting in creation of value and wealth for all stakeholders.
The Corporate Governance, which form an integral part of this Report, is set out as separate Annexure, together with the Certificate of 
compliance form Statutory Auditor of the Company V. A. Parikh & Associates LLP, Chartered Accountant regarding compliance with the 
requirements of Corporate Governance as stipulated under various regulations of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015.
Business Responsibility & Sustainability Report (BRSR)
The Securities and Exchange Board of India (“SEBI”), in May 2021, introduced sustainability-related reporting requirements in the form 
of the Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report (“BRSR”) and mandated the top 1,000 listed entities, based on market 
capitalization, to include BRSR as part of their Annual Report.
In accordance with Regulation 34(2)(f) read with Regulation 3(2A) of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations, 2015, the Company has prepared the Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report (“BRSR”) for the financial year 
2025-26. Although the Company is presently outside the top 1,000 listed entities based on market capitalization, the requirement 
continues to apply in terms of Regulation 3(2A), which provides that provisions applicable on the basis of market capitalization shall 
continue to apply unless the listed entity remains outside the applicable threshold for a period of three consecutive years.
The BRSR covers the Company's performance on Environmental, Social and Governance ("ESG") parameters for FY 2025-26 and 
includes disclosures against the nine principles of the National Guidelines on Responsible Business Conduct ("NGRBC") issued by the 
Ministry of Corporate Affairs. Cross-references have been provided, wherever appropriate, to the relevant sections of this Integrated 
Report.

Policy on Board Diversity and Director's Appointment and Remuneration Policy
The Company recognizes and embraces the importance of a diverse Board in its success. We believe that a truly diverse Board will 
leverage differences in thought, perspective, knowledge, skill, regional and industry experience, cultural and geographical 
backgrounds, age, ethnicity, race and gender that will help us retain our competitive advantage. The current policy of the board is to 
have an appropriate mix of Executive, Non-Executive and Independent Directors to maintain the independence of the Board and 
separate its functions of governance and management. As on date the Board comprises of 11 members, 3 of whom are Executive 
Directors, 2 are Non-Executive and Non-Independent Directors and 6 Independent Directors. As per regulation 17 of SEBI (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015, your Company has one Independent Women Director on its board.
The policy of the Company on directors' appointment and remuneration, including the criteria for determining qualifications, positive 
attributes, independence of a director and other matters, as required under sub-section (3) Section 178 of the Companies Act, 2013, is 
available on our website at https://www.asianstargroup.com/investor-centre/#corporate-governance.  
We affirm that the remuneration paid to the directors is as per the terms laid out in the Nomination and Remuneration Policy of the 
Company.

All new Independent Directors inducted into the Board to attend an orientation program known as Familiarisation Programme, which 
is for every new Independent Director of the Board to familiarise themselves with the strategy, operations and functions of our 
Company. The Executive Directors / Senior Managerial Personnel make presentations to the inductees about the Company's strategy, 
operations, product and service offerings, markets, organization structure, finance, human resources, technology, quality, facilities 
and risk management. In addition, the Company also keeps the Independent Directors, updated on the events and developments in the 
industry and business environment.
